金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 9 月 7 日
上週重點訊息
◼ 全球公債殖利率飆高 ， 聯準會理事緩和升息預期 ：通膨疑慮及政府債務問題推升長天期公債殖
利率。近期日本、美國、英國、德國及法國長天期公債殖利率升至多年高點； Fed 理事
Christopher Waller 表示，若通膨持續朝 2% 目標邁進，他傾向在 9 月會議按兵不動，並強
調 9 月決策將高度依賴即將公布的 8 月 CPI 數據 ， 市場解讀此為相對鴿派訊號 ， 有助緩解股 、
債市場壓力。
◼ 美伊衝突再升級：美國近期擴大對伊朗相關軍事目標的打擊行動，伊朗亦採取報復措施，市場

擔憂中東原油供應及荷姆茲海峽運輸安全受到干擾 ， 布蘭特原油價格已升至每桶95 美元附近 ，
進一步推升全球通膨預期，增加市場對未來貨幣政策路徑的不確定性。
◼ AI 行情分化，Nvidia 收購開源 AI 開發平台 Hugging Face：Snowflake 公布優於預期的財
報後股價大漲，並帶動 ServiceNow、Salesforce 及 Adobe 等軟體股走強，反觀博通雖然財
報優於預期，但第四季展望及對客製AI 晶片競爭疑慮壓抑股價表現。Nvidia 宣布以約 129 億
美元收購全球最大的開源 AI 開發平台 Hugging Face，意味 Nvidia 正由 GPU 硬體進一步延
伸至模型、資料集及 AI 開發者生態系，藉此鞏固其在全球 AI 基礎建設領域的核心地位。
市場展望與操作策略
◼ AI 題材擴散，科技股中期向上趨勢料不變：近期股市修正主要來自美債殖利率攀升，壓縮部
分科技股的評價空間，半導體族群因而承壓。然而，AI 變現題材接棒，資金由晶片端輪動至軟
體應用層，預料 AI 應用商機持續發酵。惟建議適度降低高評價、高擁擠度及高度依賴遠期獲

利族群的配置比重 。 此外 ， 投資人可透過定時定額或利用市場拉回時分批布局具獲利成長潛力
的台股及日股。
◼ 3~10 年美國公債/投資級債為債市首選：美國通膨及需求數據仍展現韌性，使市場對年底前
升息的預期再度升溫，對美國財政赤字、公債供給及長期通膨風險仍要求較高的風險溢酬。考
量目前 3 至 10 年期美國公債及投資級債可鎖定較高收益率，且受長天期財政風險影響相對較
小，因此現階段 3 至 10 年期美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構仍相對具吸引力。

日本

株式会社大和総研 丸の内オフィス 〒100-6756 東京都千代田区丸の内一丁目 9 番 1 号 グラントウキョウ ノースタワー 
このレポートは投資勧誘を意図して提供するものではありません。このレポートの掲載情報は信頼できると考えられる情報源から作成しておりますが、その正確性、完全
性を保証するものではありません。また、記載された意見や予測等は作成時点のものであり今後予告なく変更されることがあります。㈱大和総研と大和証券㈱は、㈱大和

2026 年 9 月 30 日 全7 頁

Indicators Update 
2026 年 8 月鉱工業生産 
熊本地震や台風により自動車工業が下振れ、生産全体を下押し

経済調査部 エコノミスト ビリング 安奈

[要約] 
◼ 2026 年 8 月の生産指数は前月比▲1.7％と、コンセンサス（同＋1.3％、Bloomberg 調
査）に反し、2 カ月連続で低下した。熊本地震や台風の影響で自動車工業が 減産とな
り、生産全体の下押し要因となった。 経済産業省は基調判断を「一進一退」に据え置
いた。 
◼ 先行きの生産指数は緩やかに上昇する見込み。中東情勢による悪影響が下振れリスク
になり得るものの、AI 関連需要などが押し上げ要因となり、国内生産は全体として緩
やかに増加するとみている。 
◼ 2026 年 10 月 7 日に公表予定の 8 月分の景気動向指数は、先行 CI が前月差＋1.6pt の
119.3、一致CI が同▲2.3pt の 118.3 と予想する。この予測値に基づくと、8 月の基調
判断は機械的に「足踏み」に下方修正される。

【生産】熊本地震や台風の影響で自動車工業が下振れ、生産全体が2 カ月連続で低下 
2026 年 8 月の生産指数は前月比▲1.7％と、コンセンサス（同＋1.3％、Bloomberg 調査）に
反し、2 カ月連続で低下した。経済産業省は基調判断を「一進一退」に据え置いた。 
生産指数を業種別に見ると、15 業種中 12 業種が前月から低下した。自動車工業（前月比▲
6.8％）は 4 カ月ぶりに低下した。品目別では、普通乗用車（同▲5.7％）や小型乗用車（同▲
17.3％）などが減産となった。熊本地震や台風による工場の稼働停止や部品供給の停滞が自動
車工業の減産につながり1、生産全体を下押しした。また、汎用・業務用機械工業（同▲6.0％）
は 3 カ月ぶりに低下した。品目別では、 コンベヤ（同 ▲31.2％）や運搬用クレーン（同 ▲
31.8％）などが減産となった。経済産業省「鉱工業出荷内訳表」によると、汎用・業務用機械
工業は輸出向け（同▲6.2％） 、国内向け（同▲7.3％）ともに低下した。石油・石炭製品工業
（同▲13.3％）では、ガソリン（同▲12.4％）や軽油（同▲20.7％）が減産となり、4 カ月ぶり
の低下となった。一方、生産用機械工業（同＋6.1％）や輸送機械工業（除．自動車工業） （同
＋2.9％）など3業種は前月から上昇した。生産用機械工業では金型（同＋30.0％）などが、輸
送機械工業（除．自動車工業）では航空機用発動機部品（同＋9.6％）などが増産となった。 
財別では、非耐久消費財（前月比＋0.3％）と資本財（除．輸送機械） （同＋0.2％）は上昇
した一方、生産財（同▲1.7％）と耐久消費財（同▲7.7％）、建設財（同▲6.3％）は低下した。

【出荷・在庫】出荷指数は自動車工業などを中心に2 カ月ぶりに低下 
2026 年 8 月の出荷指数は前月比▲2.5％と 2 カ月ぶりに低下した。業種別では、自動車工業
（同▲5.9％）や輸送機械工業（除．自動車工業） （同▲19.5％）など15業種中14業種が低下し
た。財別に見ると、非耐久消費財と資本財（除．輸送機械）は上昇した一方、生産財と耐久消
費財、建設財は低下した。在庫指数は同▲0.5％、在庫率指数は同＋1.7％だった。

図表 2：鉱工業の生産・出荷・在庫（左）と財別の生産（ 右）

生産予測調査による。

【先行き】生産指数は緩やかに上昇する見込み 
先行きの生産指数は、緩やかな上昇基調を辿るとみている。中東情勢による悪影響が下振れ
リスクになり得るものの、AI 関連需要などが押し上げ要因となることで、国内生産は全体とし
て緩やかに増加すると見込む。 
AI 関連財の拡大は 2024 年から日本国内の生産を押し上げてきており、今後も生産の押し上
げ要因になると見込まれる2。世界半導体市場統計（WSTS）の 2026 年春季半導体市場予測によ
れば、半導体市場は 2026 年に前年比＋89.9％と急拡大した後、2027 年も同＋26.6％の高い伸
びが見込まれている3。こうした中、一部品目を中心に AI 関連需要は引き続き生産を押し上げ
るとみている。もっとも、AI 開発のペースを減速させるかどうかは米国のテック業界で意見が
分かれており4、こうした「AI開発減速論」はリスク要因として念頭に置く必要がある。 
また、コロナ禍後の航空需要の回復を背景とした欧米航空機大手の受注残が積み上がってい
ることから、今後も航空機関連需要が国内での航空機部品生産を押し上げる可能性もある。輸
送機械工業（除．自動車工業）を中心に生産が押し上げられるとみているが、欧米航空機大手
の品質問題などにより、生産が大きく左右されやすいリスクには留意が必要だ5。 
ただし、生産の下振れリスクとして、中東情勢による悪影響は引き続き残る。原油や石油製
品の代替調達が続いているものの、供給不安はなお残る。供給不足への懸念から買いだめ等が
発生すれば価格は一段と上昇し、原材料不足を通じて生産の減少につながる可能性がある6。ま
た、中東向け輸出の停滞を通じて、国内生産が下押しされるリスクもある。 
製造工業生産予測調査では、9 月の生産指数は前月比＋3.2％と見込まれる。業種別では 11
業種中 7 業種が上昇する見通しだ。電気・情報通信機械工業（同＋11.3％）でその他の電気機
械などの増産が計画されているほか、 輸送機械工業（同＋4.6％）では乗用車の増産、生産用
機械工業（同＋6.4％）では半導体・フラットパネルディスプレイ製造装置の増産が見込まれ
ている。生産指数全体の計画のバイアスを補正した試算値（最頻値） 7で見ると、9 月は同＋
1.6％と上昇が見込まれる。 
10 月の生産指数は前月比＋3.1％と見込まれている。業種別では11 業種中 9 業種が上昇する
見通しだ。生産用機械工業（同＋12.9％）や電子部品・デバイス工業（同＋9.5％） 、電気・情
報通信機械工業（同＋9.1％）などの増産が全体を押し上げる見通しだ。

2 詳細は、神田慶司・秋元虹輝・菊池慈陽・横田凱「日本経済見通し：2026 年6 月」（大和総研レポート、
2026 年6 月23 日）を参照。 
3 WSTS “Global Semiconductor Market Surges Beyond $1.5T 2026”（2026 年6 月2 日） 
4 「アンソロピックの警告にザッカーバーグ氏反論 AI 開発減速に賛否」（日本経済新聞 電子版、2026 年9
月 17 日） 
5 詳細は、ビリング安奈「鉱工業生産は当面 AI・航空・防衛関連需要がけん引」（大和総研レポート、2026 年
9 月9 日）を参照。 
6 詳細は、畑中宏仁「ナフサ問題がもたらす日本経済の不安要素」（大和総研レポート、2026 年6 月 15 日）
を参照。 
7 生産計画は生産実績よりも上振れした値となることが多いため、生産指数全体の計画のバイアスを補正した
試算値（最頻値）が公表されている。

### Page 5

5 / 7

【26 年8 月景気動向指数】先行CI は上昇、一致CI は低下を見込む 
鉱工業指数の結果を受けて、2026 年 10 月 7 日に公表予定の 8 月分の景気動向指数は、先行
CI が前月差＋1.6pt の 119.3、一致 CI が同▲2.3pt の 118.3 と予想する（図表 4） 。先行CI で
は構成指標のうち、中小企業売上げ見通し DI や消費者態度指数、日経商品指数(42 種総合)な
どが前月から改善した。一致CIでは構成指標のうち、鉱工業用生産財出荷指数や耐久消費財出
荷指数、輸出数量指数などが悪化した。この予測値に基づくと、2026 年 8 月の基調判断は機械
的に「足踏み」に下方修正される。

図表 4：景気動向指数（先行 CI、一致 CI）と基調判断の推移

## Document 198: Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• The ongoing oil-price shock shouldn’t prove structural, with flows likely to normalize in 
2027. But several pressure points are pulling oil and gas prices in different directions 
and the balance will decide where prices go next.  Brent jumped to nearly 110 USD/bbl 
after Saudi Arabia was forced to shut its East-West pipeline, pushing the net hit to Hormuz 
flows to about 4.3mbpd vs pre -war levels.  For now, pressure points (including strategic 
reserves near historic lows  and an underinvested refining base) are outweighing the two 
relief valves (shadow traffic in Hormuz and demand destruction). As we expect the Saudi 
pipeline to be repaired rather swiftly and volumes steady in Hormuz,  Brent should average 
87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before consolidating to 76 USD/bbl in 2027. But prices would hold 
near 100 USD/bbl if the outage extends toward six weeks and Hormuz remains in flux.  
• European gas prices are climbing as the region races to refill, though the resulting 
macro risk looks contained for now. Gas prices have rallied from the mid-40 EUR/MWh in 
July to over 80 EUR/MWh as Hormuz LNG loadings stay stuck at 15-25% of pre-war levels. 
We expect TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh in Q4 2026 before easing to 36 EUR/MWh in 2027 
amid larger supply from both the US and the Middle  East. A sustained move above 120  
EUR/MWh for six months would risk tipping the Eurozone into recession.  
• A separate refining and export bottleneck is keeping refined product prices elevated. 
US and European crack spreads have surged to 62 EUR/bbl and 74 EUR/bbl. On top of 
Hormuz disruptions, a significant share of Russia’s refining capacity has been  hit by 
Ukrainian strikes, resulting in  a diesel export ban. Until June, China also had export 
restrictions in place amid fear s of supply shortage s. A structurally underinvested refining 
base is adding to the multi -layered crisis . As a result,  global oil demand is expected to 
contract 2.5mbpd in 2026 as demand from Asia has collapsed. 
• Diesel-reliant transport and agriculture, Asian petrochemicals and fertilizers are the 
most exposed sectors  with very few short -term substitutes available , while producers 
with domestic feedstock flexibility are comparatively insulated.  Between March and 
August, fossil -fuel importers paid an estimated extra USD330bn. High d iesel prices are 
hitting trucking, agriculture and construction, and the Gulf’s 40% share of global sulfur 
exports is impacting food production via fertilizer s. The n ear-term adjustment is mainly 
through inventory drawdowns and output cuts rather than genuine substitution.  
• Households are only starting to feel the full inflationary hit, and the pass -through will 
remain sticky into 2027.  In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of August’s CPI 
increase, with pump prices set to reach 3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y, about 355 USD 
more per household annually). In Europe, regulated tariffs are catching up with a lag and 
lower-income households remain disproportionately exposed.  
• Bonds are already pricing  in most of the  energy premium, but sustained inflation and 
oil-price spikes could lead to further rate stress. Much of the initial repricing has already 
occurred. We estimate that a further increase in Brent to 120 USD/bbl would only lift yields 
by 5-10bps (vs 17-18bps on inflation swaps and 8-11bps on expected policy-rate pricing). 
This suggests that a significant further rise in yields would likely require a renewed large 
supply shock or evidence that inflation is delaying monetary -policy normalization. 
• Looking ahead, over the longer run, the unfolding events  are fundamentally a 
Hormuz/Gulf shock  that will reshape global energy  markets. The conflict is reshaping 
global energy trade routes: Gulf states are building out bypass infrastructure (ADCOP, the 
Kirkuk-Ceyhan/Development Road corridor, Duqm) while China, India, Japan and South 
Korea diversify away from Gulf -transiting barrels; LNG has no equivalent bypass, so gas 
exposure remains structural. 
Ludovic Subran

Chief Economist

Chokepoints and pressure points : what is breaking 
and reshaping global energy markets

### Page 2

2

Roller-coaster oil markets  
The recent attack on Saudi Arabia’s East -West pipeline (Petroline), combined with stalled talks between Iran 
and Gulf states on Hormuz shipping, is compounding risks, but we expect the shock to fade. Brent has spiked to 
nearly 110  USD/bbl, widening an already -significant Persian Gulf supply gap (see Figure 1): despite lower Gulf 
production, exports proved more resilient, but the net hit to Hormuz flows still stands at about 4.3mbpd versus pre -
war levels. It remains uncertain when the S audi pipeline will resume normal flows of 7mbpd - a mild scenario sees 
volumes ramp back to 5-6mbpd within weeks, while a worst case keeps the pipeline shut for about six week but we 
see this as a temporary shock either way. 
Figure 1: Supply gap estimate (mbpd)

* 
additional flows  
versus pre
 -
war flows 
Sources: 
 various sources
 , Allianz Research

OECD commercial stocks remain the main shock absorber, but that buffer is thinning fast. US Strategic Petroleum 
Reserve draws have slowed to just 0.3mbpd over the past month - reserves are now near their lowest level in 
decades, so Washington has little scope to repeat the scale of releases seen in 2022  or earlier this year even if the 
deficit widens further ; while Japan and South Korea are rebuilding their own reserves rather than drawing them 
down. China’s crude imports, meanwhile, are only just restarting after months of weakness and remain about 
4mbpd below year-ago levels; refiners are taking advantage of firm margins and spare quota to buy and reprocess 
crude for re-export rather than to meet stronger domestic demand, so this is not yet a genuine demand recovery. 
Against this backdrop, we forecast Brent averaging 88 USD/bbl in Q3 and 87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before easing to 
76 USD/bbl in 2027 - but a deeper or more prolonged Saudi export disruption, without an offsetting reserve release, 
would keep the market in deficit for longer and raise the probability of Brent holding near 100  USD/bbl.

A natural gas squeeze looming over Europe? 
Natural gas prices are ticking up as Europe races to refill storage.  European gas is exposed to the same Middle 
East disruption through a different channel: LNG loadings through Hormuz have remained stuck at 15 -25% of pre-
war levels for two months, even as Qatar sends more empty tankers into the Gulf. We expect Qatari and Emirati 
LNG exports to normalize only in Q1 2027, so for TTF to keep pricing enough Asian LNG demand out of the market 
to free cargoes for Europe, prices must stay elevated for longer: the rally from the mid -40 EUR/MWh in July to over 
80 EUR/MWh has already cut some demand from the rest of the world. With Europe still playing catch -up on 
storage (see Figure 2), we expect TTF to average 65 EUR/MWh in both Q3 and Q4 2026 before consolidating to 36 
EUR/MWh in 2027 as Hormuz flows normalize and incremental US LNG supply arrives. The key near -term risk is 
storage: a cold winter combined with a lower-than-usual fill could deplete storage before spring, front-loading price 
pressure into early 2027. 
Figure 2: European gas storage

Sources: AGSI, Allianz Research  
as of Sept
 . 14

### Page 4

4

For now, the macro risk from gas prices looks contained.  In 2022, gas prices averaged close to 130 EUR/MWh for 
a full year, yet the Eurozone still grew about 3.4%, cushioned by a post -pandemic demand boom and a fiscal 
response worth an estimated 0.4pp of growth. Today’s backdrop is less forgiving: fiscal space has narrowed and 
household demand is far less dynamic. We think TTF in the 80 -100 EUR/MWh range could be absorbed for nine 
months or more without a growth shock, but a sustained move above 120 EUR/MWh for six months or longer would 
tip the Eurozone into recession. Our base case, TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh through Q3 and Q4 2026, does not test 
that threshold, though a winter upside scenario would keep the tail risk alive.  
Refined products to remain expensive  
A two -layer infrastructure bottleneck is hitting refined products and will keep driving regional prices up, 
independent of crude.  In August, Brent averaged 89  USD/bbl, yet clean -product crack spreads surged to record 
levels, the US benchmark reached 62  EUR/bbl and European diesel crack 74 EUR/bbl (68% and 92% above their 
five-year averages) showing the main story is refining availability, not crude input costs. Three regions are driving 
the crunch. In the Middle East, the US -Iran conflict and Hormuz blockade have hit both refinery operations and 
product exports: pre -conflict flows of roughly  6mbpd through the Strait have fallen to 1.1mbpd, and the Saudi 
pipeline attack compounds this by also halting flows to the Yanbu refineries. In Russia, Ukrainian strikes cut refinery 
runs to multi -year lows by mid -May, and Moscow’s diesel export ban - extended through September, with no 
guarantee against further extension - has squeezed a market that depends on Russia for 11% of global diesel. In 
China, export restrictions imposed in March to preserve domestic inventories cut clean -product exports by 24.3% 
year-on-year over the first five months, though Beijing’s June release of 13mn tons should ease global supply, lifting 
China’s exports full-year total to 32mn tons, 0.6% above last year. 
Figure 3: US and Europe crack spread historical prices (USD/bbl)

Sources: 
 LSEG Workspace
 , Allianz Research

Global clean-product inventories are drying up as the crisis extends through 2026.  European (ARA) inventories 
are well below the five -year average. In the US, refiners are running at record utilization (98% in September), but 
most of the extra output is being exported for higher margins rather than sold domestically — gasoline stocks are 
6% and diesel 14% below seasonal norms, and US diesel topped 6  USD/gallon for the first time. Rising domestic 
prices could eventually pull barrels back into the US market and curb these exports, with some early – tough strongly 
pushed back by government officials for now  - talks about potential diesel export restrictions to support domestic 
supply. Additionally, there is little relief from spare capacity: years of underinvestment and refinery closures have 
left the West structurally unprepared, global refining investment is on track to fall below 40  USD bn this year (-15% 
y/y), and capacity is set to grow only marginally through 2040. Unlike 2022, when Europe and Asia reallocated 
capital toward LNG regasification within months, refining capacity cannot be added quickly enough to ease this 
shortage in the short or medium term, and rebuilding inventories will take time even once Hormuz flows normalize. 
We now expect world oil demand to contract by 2.5mbpd in 2026 as high product prices force further demand 
destruction, concentrated in middle distillates and petrochemical feedstocks in Asia. Longer term, persistently 
expensive products should accelerate substitution toward EVs and heat pumps, and put a premium on refineries 
with the flexibility to shift crude grades and product mix.

Downstream sector impact and potential substitution 
After six months, duration, and not just the peak price, is now the central macro risk. Between March and August, 
fossil-fuel importers paid an estimated 330  USD bn more than pre-war forward prices implied, split roughly evenly 
between crude and refined products - a real income transfer from importers to exporters that squeezes household 
purchasing power and corporate margins, weakens currencies, and strains public finances wherever governments 
subsidize fuel. The Hormuz crisis is unusual in disrupting many linked -but-hard-to-substitute commodities at once 
(crude, gas, LPG, naphtha and sulfur ) spilling into fertilizer and petrochemical shortages. Substitution can happen 
at three levels : (i) switching energy carriers, (ii) switching industrial feedstocks, or (iii) consumers switching final 
products. With the first working fastest but simply redistributing scarcity, and the other two developing more slowly 
but reshaping industrial structure permanently. Inflation itself spreads in three stages: a fast first -round hit to 
energy-intensive industries (speed depending on taxes and subsidies), a slower production -cost effect as old-price 
inventories and contracts run out, and a second -round effect as wages, inflation expectations and price -setting 
behavior adjust. 
The immediate impact is on energy -intensive industries.  Gulf states are major exporters of both fuels and 
feedstocks: Europe is less directly exposed to Gulf crude than East Asia but still feels it through global oil prices, LNG 
competition and product shortages; the US is more insulated on crude but exposed via global product prices, hitting 
lower-income consumers hardest while producers and refiners gain from higher margins. East and South Asia face 
the largest physical exposure, with China, India and Japan recording the highest net additional fossil -fuel import 
costs since the conflict began – USD 31.7 bn, USD 14.4bn and USD 10.4bn respectively. Diesel is the most powerful 
transmission channel given its role in trucking, agriculture, mining and construction - sectors that are hard to 
electrify, especially for long -haul transport and in emerging markets - while shipping and aviation face higher 
bunker/jet-fuel costs and rerouting. Among industrial inputs, East Asian petrochemical crackers reliant on imported 
naphtha are most exposed, whereas the US benefits from domestic ethane and China retains partial cover via coal-
based chemicals; metals, cement and glass are exposed through electricity and high-temperature heat costs, hitting 
aluminum hardest. Gulf countries supply more than 40% of global sulfur exports and about 20% of 
ammonia/nitrogen-fertilizer exports, so the crisis strikes food production through fertilizer, diesel, irrigation and 
packaging costs simultaneously. 
The second-round effect on the broader economy will be felt with a lag and last longer.  Food shows this best: 
packaged food embeds fertilizer, farm fuel, irrigation, refrigeration, processing heat, packaging, transport and 
labor, each repricing at a different speed : fresh produce reacts within weeks, grain can take up to a year via fertilizer 
and harvest effects, and restaurant prices adjust slowest but most persistently as labor and rent costs catch up. 
Large retailers can hedge and absorb costs better than small one s, and real household income falls as consumers 
trade down and defer spending. Export restrictions, subsidies and price controls can temporarily shield domestic 
food prices but tend to redistribute scarcity and worsen the burden on lower -income, food-importing economies. 
0
10
20
30
40
2020 2025 2030 2035 2039
Asia North America Europe Middle East

### Page 6

6

Construction and real estate are hit both directly (cement, plastics, metals, diesel) and, more durably, indirectly 
through higher interest rates raising financing costs and lowering the present value of future rents. Compared with 
2022, the economy enters this shock with more renewable capacity and European LNG infrastructure - renewables 
installed since 2020 reportedly avoided USD 36bn of fossil -fuel imports in the first five months of the crisis - but 
public debt is higher and buffers are thinner after the pandemic and the 2022 crisis.  
Substitution will spread across fuels, feedstocks and products but initially redistributes inflation rather than 
eliminating it. The first response is inventory drawdowns, switching to costlier available inputs, output cuts and cost 
pass-through. Over time it becomes more structural, reorganizing downstream markets around flexibility, lower 
energy intensity and redundancy - persistently expensive oil and gas improve the economics of electrification, even 
though electrification changes commodity dependence rather than removing it. Because capital, regulatory 
approval and margins all constrain the pace of change, expect a two -stage adjustment: curtailment and demand 
destruction first, structural substitution and capex later , alongside rising demand for fuel/feedstock flexibility even 
where it costs more than a specialized, single-fuel plant. 
Figure 5a and 5b: Fuel potential substitution in energy-intensive sectors in the short-term; energy-intensive sector 
inputs (left) within share of total inputs in specific sectors (% - right)

Source: Allianz Research
 , Eurostat, Oxford Economics  
Households to face higher inflation 
Six months in, households are losing purchasing power in stages.  Petrol and diesel reprice within days or weeks, 
but gas and electricity bills follow regulated tariff calendars and contract -renewal dates, so the current inflation 
reading is not yet the peak. Exposure varies sharply by household - an EV- and heat-pump-owning homeowner is 
comparatively insulated, a gas-heated renter won’t feel the full effect until tariffs reset, and a rural household reliant 
on diesel cars feels it immediately. Petrol and diesel are the fastest channel: European pu mp prices typically pass 
through crude costs within one to two months, roughly one -for-one. In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of 
August’s CPI increase, diesel peaked above 6  USD/gallon in September, and average pump prices are on track for 
3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y) - about USD 355 more per year for a typical household; hitting rural, low-income 
households hardest given limited access to public transport.  
Gas and electricity bills rise on institutional timetables, so the pass -through is felt with a lag that varies by 
market. In France, the regulated gas reference price rises 6.3% on October 1 (to 182.88  EUR/MWh including tax, 
about 60 EUR/year for indexed contracts), though roughly 40% of households are on fixed-price contracts and won’t 
feel it until renewal; French electricity moves more slowly, with the next adjustment scheduled for February 2027. 
The UK’s quarterly price cap rises by a bout 5  GBP/month from October, partly offset by government VAT relief, 
while roughly 35% of households on fixed contracts are shielded for now. Japan and India rely more on subsidy 
calendars than market pass -through, which smooths the household shock across time wit hout removing its fiscal 
cost. More broadly, real energy bills were still about 4% above 2019 levels in 2024, so this shock lands on budgets 
that had not fully recovered from 2022 ; and unevenly: an estimated 120mn households in advanced economies 
spend over 10% of income on home energy, rising to 20% including transport for the poorest, and the bottom income 
quintile spent 12% of disposable income on gas, electricity and heating in 2022 versus 4% for the top quintile.

In the longer term, the energy crisis impacts inflation beyond energy bills.  A second inflationary wave builds as 
transport and industrial costs feed into food, goods and services with a lag of around two years: a permanent 10% 
rise in oil prices raises headline CPI by about 2.3%, food CPI by 0.3% and core CPI by 0.1%, and after e xpenditure 
weights, headline CPI rises by almost 0.4% - consistent with the roughly 0.5% inflation contribution the last oil move 
added in the Eurozone, UK and Canada. Looking ahead, households are likely to weigh energy resilience more 
heavily in purchasing decisions, shifting spending from variable fuel cost s toward upfront capex on electrification 
and insulation - even as higher prices simultaneously squeeze the ability to finance that switch, widening inequality 
in energy exposure. Electricity is set to become the central household energy price, trading oil and gas exposure for 
exposure to power prices, network ch arges and time -of-use pricing, with government subsidies and price -cap 
calendars still deciding when the crisis is actually felt.

The energy-yield loop 
Energy shocks often have an immediate effect on rates through several components.  The first one is inflation 
expectations: Higher commodity prices tend to increase inflation and are priced directly on the derivatives market . 
Historically a 1% rise in Brent lifts the 2Y inflation swap by 1. 65bps in the US and 1. 77bps in the Eurozone, and the 
1y1y forward inflation swap by 0.64bp in the US and 1.09bp in the Eurozone. During special shocks the effect 
intensifies: The US 2Y inflation swap moved 1. 86bps per 1% of oil during the Ukraine shock and 2. 01bps during the 
Iran episode. The EU 2Y inflation swap moved by 2.60bps during the Ukraine shock, but only 0.36bp during the Iran 
episode. 
The first channel then impacts  the expected short rates path.  When inflation tends to increase suddenly, the 
central bank faces more pressure  to be hawkish because of market pricing . Historically a 1% rise in Brent adds 
1.02bp to US monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y OIS − Fed Funds) and 0.83bp to EUR monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y 
OIS − €STR). During crises , this pressure concentrates region by region: US pricing repriced hardest during the 
Ukraine shock at 2.82bps per 1% of oil, close to three times its long -run sensitivity, while in the current Iran episode

it is EUR pricing that dominates at 2.40bps. This second channel tends to move short rates (more sensitive to inflation 
and monetary policy) (<2Y maturities: US 2Y +0.95bp, DE 2Y +0.53bp per 1% of oil) and the "belly" (more sensitive 
to growth and the economy) (5Y –7Y: US 5Y +0.98bp, DE 5Y +0.54bp) fa ster than long rates (which embed more 
fundamental factors such as demography, fiscal situation, political stability) (>10Y maturity: US 30Y +0.65bp, DE 
30Y +0.50bp). When the front end increases more than the long end, it induces a bear flattening , which hurts the 
economy more broadly. This tends to tighten financial conditions by increasing borrowing costs, slowing the growth 
of production and credit, but also by increasing transport costs, making it harder for commodities trading houses to 
provide more inputs to markets due to their short -term financing at 3M.

In times of shocks, the sensitivities of commodities on rates roughly double . On the German curve, a 1% rise in 
Brent moves the 2Y yield by 0.53bp historically versus 1.43bp in the current episode, and EUR monetary pricing by 
0.83bp versus 2.40bp. Per +10% move in Brent, that is about +5bps on the German curve and +9bps on the US curve 
in normal times, rising to about +14bps on the German 2Y and +12bps on the US 10Y in shock, with the 2Y inflation 
swaps moving some + 17bps historically and up to + 20bps ina  shock.  Figure 9 illustrates the incremental impact 
on rates and inflation markets implied by a move in Brent to USD120, USD100 or USD80 from current levels. 
These scenario moves are separate from the repricing already observed during the current episode, much of 
which is already embedded in inflation compensation and monetary-policy expectations.

### Page 9

9

Bond markets have already absorbed a large portion of the current energy shock through higher inflation 
compensation and a repricing of the expected monetary -policy path. While additional oil-price gains would still 
exert upward pressure on rates, the scenario analysis suggests that the marginal impact is now smaller than during 
the initial phase of the shock. As a result, a sustained and significant further rise in yields would likely require a new 
escalation in supply disruptions or clearer evidence that energy -driven inflation is feeding into broader price 
dynamics and delaying the normalization of monetary policy.  
How the conflict is reshaping the global energy map 
The Strait of Hormuz has always been the Gulf’s structural vulnerability, and this crisis is accelerating a shift 
from managing chokepoint risk to actively engineering around it.  The attack on Saudi Arabia’s own hedge (i.e.  
Petroline) is a reminder that existing bypass infrastructure is now a target, not a guarantee. Riyadh’s repair timeline 
will determine how quickly that redundancy returns, but the episode has already revived talk of expanding capacity 
rather than just repairing it. The UAE’s ADCOP pipeline to Fujairah, which sits outside the Strait entirely, remains the 
cleanest working bypass and has absorbed a large share of redirected flows, with further capacity additions 
planned for 2027. Iraq is leaning on the Kirkuk -Ceyhan pipeline and the broader Development Road corridor to 
Türkiye, while Oman’s Duqm port is the other candidate for a Gulf pipeline that bypasses the Strait altogether.

There is no easy fix for natural gas.  Unlike crude, LNG cannot be pipelined around Hormuz, which is why Qatari 
and Emirati LNG loadings have collapsed even as oil exports partially recovered via redirection. With normalization 
pushed out to Q1 2027, Qatar’s pattern of sending more empty tanke rs into the Gulf is one of the few signs of an 
eventual ramp-up, but there is no Petroline-equivalent for gas and the exposure is structural. 
On the demand side, the pressure to de -risk predates this crisis — it dates back to the war in Ukraine — and is 
now intensifying, though Russia/Ukraine plays no direct role in the current Hormuz shock beyond the refined -
products channel already noted.  China has spent the past decade building overland alternatives (the ESPO 
pipeline from Russia and the Kazakhstan -China link) specifically to cut seaborne Gulf and Malacca dependency, 
and its crude imports remain about 4mbpd below 2025 levels even at firm prices — though the recent uptick in 
Chinese buying is a refining -margin and re -export story more than fresh demand. India is diversifying toward US, 
West African and discounted Russian barrels via the Cape route while expanding strategic reserves, and Japan and 
South Korea are rebuilding SPRs drawn down earlier in the crisis. The India -Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor 
is likely to see fresh political momentum as a longer-term, non-maritime link between the Gulf and Europe. None of

### Page 10

10

this displaces Hormuz as the region’s central artery near -term, but it marks a durable shift toward supply -side 
redundancy and demand-side diversification that will outlast the current conflict.

为何暂缓发布 GPT-6.1 Astra ？

司⽤于评估安全性和对⻬度的部分测试标准，⽽⾮遇到了什么具体的严重事故。

从更⼴泛的层⾯来看， Altman 表⽰，随着模型能⼒的提升，对⻬、安全、监控和防护必须始
终 " 远远领先于模型的能⼒ " 。他将这种⽅法称为对发展节奏的 " 调控 " 。
AI安全挑战超越了⼯程范畴

安全系统并⾮ " 完整的解决⽅案 " ；AI 更多的是⼀个科学问题，⽽⾮⼯程问题； OpenAI 将拥

有 " 许多伟⼤的新模型 " 。

作外，⾏业还⾯临着科学层⾯的挑战。
关于法律责任， Altman 预计将会出现⼀个多层级的责任框架，具体取决于故障是源于模型本

⽂件还指出，云服务商可以看到 Anthropic 的定价和商业条款，这可能影响他们在算⼒分配和产
品推⼴⼒度上的决策。

图⽚
两⼤客⼾各占 12% ，且⽆⻓期合同
客⼾集中度同样是⼀⼤⻛险。
招股书显⽰，有两家未具名客⼾各⾃贡献了 Anthropic 12%的年营收。
Anthropic 警告，其多数⼤客⼾并不受⻓期合同约束，随时可能削减或停⽌⽀出。
在应收账款层⾯，截⾄ 2025 年底， Anthropic 共有9.09 亿美元未收客⼾账款，其中60%由亚⻢逊
和⾕歌代为收取，⾼于 2024 年的 42% 。 Anthropic 提⽰，⼀旦这⼀环节出现争议或延迟，即便公
司与客⼾直接签有合同，现⾦流仍可能受到冲击。
与 OpenAI 的收⼊⼝径之争
这份招股书还将 Anthropic 与 OpenAI 之间的收⼊统计⼝径之争再度推⾄台前。
Anthropic 将云平台市场合同的全额计⼊营收，将平台分成计为营销成本 —— 依据是 Anthropic 作

为交易 “ 主要⽅ ” 负责定价和服务交付，符合会计准则。
但据路透社今年 6 ⽉的报道， OpenAI 曾向投资者和员⼯表⽰，这种处理⽅式使 Anthropic 的营收
虚增了数⼗亿美元。
对此， Anthropic 回应称，公司遵循既定会计准则，确认总收⼊是因为其在交易中是 “ 主要⽅ ” 。

## Document 209: Asia week ahead_ Indian rate decision, key data on Philippines, Japan, Taiwan

Source PDF: all report pdf/Asia week ahead_ Indian rate decision, key data on Philippines, Japan, Taiwan.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Reserve Bank of India is widely expected to deliver a 25bp
rate hike. Markets will also be watching inﬂation data from the
Philippines, household spending and external-sector ﬁgures
from Japan, and inﬂation and trade releases from Taiwan

Deepali Bhargava and Lynn Song
Asia Research highlights of the week
China PMIs return to expansion territory, signalling modest growth
uptick
RBA stays focused on inﬂation, signals more tightening if needed
China’s State Council suggests more policy support ahead
Xi-Trump summit: Pandas and positive vibes but limited progress
India: RBI expected to hike rate by 25bp
The Reserve Bank of India is likely to begin its tightening cycle on
Wednesday with a 25bp hike in its policy rate to 5.5%, after holding
rates steady since December. Rising oil and a sharp pickup in food

### Page 2

prices are expected to push CPI inﬂation back above the RBI's target
range in the fourth quarter. While the inﬂation shock is largely
supply-driven, we expect the RBI to raise rates to prevent inﬂation
expectations from de-anchoring. Markets will be focused on the
RBI's guidance on the extent and duration of the tightening cycle.
Philippines: Inﬂation to rise on food and fuel
pressures
Fuel and El Niño-related food price pressures are likely to push

Philippine inﬂation higher to 6.8% year-on-year in September, from
6.1% in August. Domestic fuel prices rose by more than 15% during
the month, while adverse weather conditions continue to drive up
food costs, particularly rice.
Japan: Wages, spending, external balances
in focus
Japan releases a range of household and external-sector data.
Market consensus sees consumer conﬁdence edging down to 35.2
in September. Labour cash earnings growth is forecast to moderate

to 3.8% YoY in August, while real cash earnings are expected to
remain positive but slow to 1.6% YoY. The current account surplus is
expected to widen to JPY 3150bn, supported by overseas
investment income, despite the trade deﬁcit widening to JPY
753.1bn. Meanwhile, household spending is expected to remain
weak, declining 3.6% YoY, highlighting continued pressure on
domestic consumption.
Taiwan: Export growth to remain strong
Taiwan releases September inﬂation and trade data. Market

consensus expects headline CPI inﬂation to accelerate to 2.4% YoY,
from 2.04% in August. Export growth is forecast to strengthen to
47.3% YoY, from 41.0%, supported by continued demand for
semiconductors and other electronic products. Imports are seen
remaining broadly stable at 44.0% YoY, compared with 44.3%
previously. The trade surplus is forecast to narrow to $19.2bn, from
$22.3bn in August.

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia,
fell 1.1% in September with downward revisions to the
previous two months again shaving a further 0.4% off prices.
The correction has now seen prices decline 5.2% from their

March peak. Annual growth is flat.
The price correction remains broad, Cotality reporting 97% of
capital city suburbs experienced falls over the three months
to September, Darwin the only city to record gains. Sydney
and Melbourne continue to see more pronounced declines,
albeit moderating a touch, particularly in Melbourne.
Against this, declines in Brisbane, Adelaide and Perth have
accelerated over the last few months. Across segments, unit
prices and lower price tiers continue to track slightly milder

falls than houses and top tiers.
Seasonal variations accentuated price weakness through winter
but are now neutral. The peak-to-trough price decline nationally
is slightly milder at 4.6% in seasonally adjusted terms. Unless
specified, figures in titles, tables and the commentary below are
in non-seasonally adjusted terms, consistent with the ‘headline’
figures reported by Cotality. However, figures shown in charts
are in seasonally adjusted terms.

Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 3.9%qtr in September and 17.4%yr with previous
estimates revised 1-2ppts lower. The monthly pattern
suggests small gains in July and August have given way to
another sizeable fall in September.
Looking at the price performance across different jurisdictions:
• Sydney prices dropped 1.4% fall in September, monthly
declines averaging 1.6% over the previous four months,
tracking a 17% annualised pace of decline since May. All

segments and tiers recorded falls in Q3 but with top and
middle tier houses notably weaker.
• Melbourne prices fell 0.7%mth, a slower pace than other
capitals. This likely reflects the weaker starting point coming
into the correction, prices already back to the levels seen in
mid-2021. As elsewhere, 'top tier' houses prices are leading
the decline. 'Low tier' unit prices mostly held their ground in
Q3, prices in this sub-segment already plumbing 10yr lows.

• Brisbane was the weakest capital city price-wise, recording
a 1.5% drop in September with previous monthly declines
also revised down heavily (August from 1% to 1.6% and
July from 1.2% to 1.7%). The combined effect has taken
the rolling 3mth change to –4.7%qtr from –2.7%qtr last
month and –0.6%qtr in July. At 5.9%yr, annual growth is
still positive but losing altitude quickly, having run at 18%yr
as recently as May. Sellers have been slow to respond to

the sudden stop with total listings up over 50% vs a year
ago, albeit still not high compared to long run averages
(reflecting the extremely tight starting point for the Brisbane
market).
• Adelaide dwelling prices fell 1.3%mth, the August decline

also marked down from 0.8% to 1.1%, annual price growth
dropping to 6.5%yr. The price correction began later in
Adelaide but is now running at a similar pace to prices
nationally.
• Perth recorded a 1.2% decline but also saw big downward
revisions to previous months (August from 0.8% to –1.7%
and July from –1.3% to –1.8%). At 10.1%yr, annual growth
is still solidly positive but was tracking at over 25%yr earlier
in the year. As with Adelaide and Brisbane, the detail shows

a more even spread of declines across price tiers. However,
turnover is showing a milder pull-back in Adelaide.
• The smaller capitals were again more mixed, price growth
still positive in Darwin (0.4%mth, 11.9%yr), relatively
resilient in Hobart (–0.5%mth, 7%yr) but weaker in
Canberra (–1.1%mth, –1.6%yr).
• Regional markets showed a 0.9% decline in Qld but slightly
milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.3%). Annual
prices growth is in the 3.4-6.3%yr range.

Our recently released Housing Pulse report (here) provides
a deeper diver into the situation in Australia's housing
markets. While the absence of selling pressure, still tight
supply-demand balance and sentiment mix suggests the
correction was still relatively 'orderly' through the September
quarter, the latest monthly data shows momentum remains
unambiguously negative.
That said, there are clearly more headwinds to come. The
RBA latest interest rate rise and what now looks to be a

more likely prospect of a follow-up increase at its meeting in
early November will impact in coming months. Next week's
consumer sentiment update will show how the change, and
the latest round of weak updates, is shaping housing-related
sentiment. Either way, momentum looks set to remain weak
into year-end and, barring a significantly improved inflation
and interest rate outlook, early 2027.

## Document 233: Cliff Notes_ searching for signal (2026-10-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ searching for signal (2026-10-02).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: searching for signal
By Sian Fenner, Ryan Wells & Illiana Jain
13:33 October 02 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia, this week’s main event was the RBA Monetary Policy Board’s (MPB) decision to raise the
cash rate by 25bps to 4.60%. This outcome was universally expected given Governor Bullock had
already communicated that upside risks to inflation had materialised – the conditionality established by

the MPB back in August for considering another rate hike.

### Page 2

Governor Bullock made explicit note of three upside risks. The Middle East conflict was emphasised as
the risk that had “materialised”, given the re-escalation and renewed surge in crude oil and refined
product prices over recent months. The RBA’s liaison warns this could lead to further pass-through of
costs and more persistent inflation pressures. On top of this, domestic capacity pressures and the data
centre/AI investment boom were also noted as upside risks that the MPB remain alert to.

In a video update mid-week, Chief Economist Luci Ellis explains why the bar for a follow-up rate hike in
November is low. We now expect a 25bp increase in November, provided there is no sudden resolution
of the Middle East conflict beforehand, or some other event that significantly lowers the outlook for
energy-related costs.
Subsequently, the August CPI revealed that underlying (trimmed mean) inflation rose 0.2% (3.4%yr),
which was slightly below the market’s expectation but spot on Westpac’s forecast. As usual, the

monthly detail was mixed, but the key drivers of underlying inflation remain centred on housing-related
components, particularly rents and new dwelling purchases. Headline inflation remained volatile, with
the unwind of the fuel excise cut causing another spike in fuel prices. We have maintained our forecast
for a 0.9% lift in trimmed mean inflation in Q3, and with upside risks building for Q4, a follow-up rate
hike in November appears most likely.

Regarding the current growth pulse, household spending surprised with a flat reading in August.
Services spend fell back following recent strength that was driven by major sporting events, but this
was offset by an increase in goods spend, particularly in fuel and electric vehicles. Together with our
latest card data for early September, the consumer spending pulse is shading a touch softer. However,
another solid outcome for business investment could once again be on the cards for Q3. August’s

goods trade data showcased another significant spike in data centre-related equipment imports, up
$4.3bn, similar in scale to Q1. While clearly import-driven, our modelling suggests that the local
economic impact of such investment is likely to be around half of the total investment.
Before moving offshore, a final note on housing. Cotality’s latest update revealed that dwelling prices
fell another 1.1% nationally in September. While Sydney and Melbourne have led the correction to

date, the fact that the other capitals are also reporting price declines clearly indicates that the
correction is deepening and broadening, as higher interest rates collide with the tax policy changes
announced in May’s budget. Sentiment effects indeed remain at play, as evinced by the continued pull-
back in investor credit growth. Westpac anticipates a peak-to-trough decline of around 7%, with tight
on-market supply limiting the extent of the correction. For more, see our latest Housing Pulse.

Offshore, there was plenty of data to dissect.
In the US, inflation eased in August despite ongoing strength in household demand and higher energy
prices. Headline PCE inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.4%yr, while core PCE increased 0.2%mth and
3.0%yr. Encouragingly for the FOMC, the share of PCE components rising by more than 3% over the
past year, a measure frequently cited by Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh, declined to 45% from 49%,
suggesting some easing in broad-based inflation pressures. At the same time, household spending

remained robust, increasing 0.9%mth, equivalent to around 0.6%mth in real terms. That said, personal
income growth was soft, rising 0.2% in the month, signalling households opted to draw down savings.

### Page 3

The ISM manufacturing survey was stronger than the headline suggested. While the index edged down
0.1pt to 54.5, manufacturing has now expanded for nine consecutive months. There were some signs
of capacity constraints emerging, with production softened in the month but backlogs up 4.6pts to 56.4,
suggesting that firms struggled to keep pace with demand. New orders rose 1.6pts to 55.3, primarily
due to domestic demand as new export orders eased, backlogs increased. Firms responded by lifting

employment, 1.5pts to 52.7. Against this backdrop manufacturers saw a renewed acceleration in cost
pressures, with the prices paid index jumping 6.8pts to 77.9. Firms cited higher steel and aluminium
prices, elevated energy costs, rising freight charges and ongoing semiconductor shortages, with every
major industry reporting higher input costs during the month.
The past week’s data will have done nothing to bridge the clear divide across the FOMC, as evident in

this week’s busy Fedspeak. Some participants argued for further rate hikes, citing strong inflation,
diminished supply capacity and policy that is not yet restrictive. Others counselled patience, pointing to
progress on inflation and that September’s hike buys time to assess upcoming data. The one thing
both agree on is that inflation will be the primary determinant of the timing and extent of further rate
hikes. Our view remains that one further hike is likely this cycle to bring policy closer to neutral.

In China, policymakers announced another round of targeted support measures aimed at stabilising
growth and supporting the struggling property sector. The PBOC lowered rates on its Pledged
Supplementary Lending facility by 25bps to 1.5% and expanded quotas for targeted lending programs.
Authorities also introduced subsidies for eligible first-home buyers. The subsidy applies to homes with
a floor area of 120m² or less and a value below CNY1.5mn, with support capped at 1ppt of mortgage

costs, up to CNY10,000 and for a maximum term of five years. The measures complement reforms
announced in August to improve developer financing conditions and support housing completion.
However, weak credit demand and confidence remains a significant constraint, suggesting policy
transmission is still limited.
The September PMI data pointed to a modest improvement in activity, although the details were less
convincing. Manufacturing returned to expansionary territory, rising 0.9pts to 50.1, supported by

stronger production and new orders. Much of the improvement, however, reflected a rebound in prices,
with the raw materials price index rising 7.6pts and producer prices increasing 6.2pts. Labour market
conditions remained weak, with the employment index falling a further 0.6pts and remaining below 50
for almost four years. Conditions in the non-manufacturing sector also improved, with the services PMI
lifting from 49.0 to 50.2, driven largely by stronger construction activity. Input and output prices moved

higher, selling prices returned back above 50, export orders improved to 48.5 and employment
increased 0.5pts, although at 45.9 it remains firmly in contractionary territory. Overall, the surveys
suggest activity is stabilising and policy support is gaining some traction, but weak labour demand,
subdued credit growth and soft domestic demand indicate the recovery remains fragile.
In Japan, the September Tankan survey delivered another constructive signal on the economy and

reinforced the case for further policy normalisation. Business conditions improved by 2pts for large
manufacturers and 5pts more broadly, while profitability indicators strengthened and capital
expenditure intentions remained elevated. Large manufacturers expect fixed investment to increase by
11.6% in FY2026, while large non-manufacturers expect investment growth of 11.2%, pointing to

### Page 4

Related articles
continued confidence in the medium-term outlook.
Capacity indicators improved further across both manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors, with
measures of excess production capacity becoming less negative and indicating that demand remains
sufficiently strong to keep pressure on existing capacity. Labour shortages also intensified, with
employment conditions becoming more negative across most industries and firm sizes, pointing to a

progressively tighter labour market. Inflation expectations remained anchored around 2%, providing
further evidence that Japan is moving towards a more sustainable inflation environment. Overall, the
survey was consistent with an economy operating close to capacity and supports the view that a
December rate hike remains a realistic possibility.

## Document 263: Five reasons why French growth should pick up (2026-09-29)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Five reasons why French growth should pick up (2026-09-29).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-29
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.35 / 28 September 2026EDITORIAL
FIVE REASONS WHY FRENCH GROWTH SHOULD PICK UP
1 We will review this in an EcoFlash scheduled for publication on 1st October.
2 The municipal election cycle (around which local investment always contracts)
has weighed on the economy, as well as the decline in agricultural production (more
heavily than elsewhere in the Eurozone, notably due to the weight of corn production

in France).
3 This has led to additional expenditure: debt servicing (indexed bonds made
more expensive by rebounding inflation) and other spending (fuel subsidies and
the budget for the military's external operations).
In 2026, the government once again failed to stabilise its public debt-to-GDP ratio due to a significant fiscal deficit and
lower-than-usual growth. According to our analysis, this underperformance is due to exceptional factors. Structurally,

France can still generate growth that will help the public debt ratio to stabilise, provided that its budget deficit is brought
down to approximately 3% of GDP . To achieve this, it has significant strengths: 1/ Improved competitiveness in terms of la-
bour and energy costs; 2/ Its services sector, notably through the development and use of new technologies (including AI);
3/ An industrial recovery driven by aeronautics, military spending and electrification, which calls for increased invest-

ment; 4/ Accelerating business creation that supports employment; 5/ Its attractiveness to foreign investors. All of these
structural drivers should enable French growth to rebound and join the expected acceleration in the Eurozone in 2027.
The French government will present its 2027 draft budget 1, while
the presidential election looms on the horizon. Ahead of these major
events, the French people are appearing very pessimistic about the
picture in France. The latest survey by the Jean Jaurès Foundation re -

veals that nearly two out of three people use only negative words, with
"worry" and "uncertainty" at the forefront. Furthermore, three-quar -
ters expect the country's situation to deteriorate.
Indeed, the French economy has faced a series of setbacks since the
beginning of the year. It narrowly avoided recession in the first half
(a 0.2% q/q contraction of GDP in Q1 and stagnation in Q2), in contrast
to the Eurozone, where growth excluding Ireland remained stable

(0.3% q/q per quarter).
Even though exceptional factors are behind this underperformance 2,
it is weighing on public finances (lower revenues), just like another
exceptional factor: the conflict in Iran 3. Consequently, the fiscal deficit
is expected to increase to 5.4% of GDP in 2026 (compared to 5.1% in
2025), according to the government. According to our forecasts, the pu-
blic debt-to-GDP ratio would reach 119% in 2026, compared to 115.7%
in 2025.

Against this backdrop, France's ability to generate growth is crucial.
Yet, even with fiscal consolidation which should, by its very nature,
weigh on demand, we can identify five reasons to believe that the
French economy can generate growth significantly above 2026 outco -
me (0.5% according to our forecasts).
1. FRANCE IS MORE COMPETITIVE
During the Eurozone crisis (2012–13), France implemented a policy
to reduce labour costs through the Competitiveness and Employment

Tax Credit (CICE). This was subsequently replaced, at the beginning of
Emmanuel Macron's first term, by a 6-pp reduction in social security
contributions. These labour-cost improvements persist today, whereas
we have seen an acceleration over the last decade in wage growth
elsewhere in Europe, notably in Germany. Alongside the relative impro-
vement in French labour costs, the bilateral trade deficit with Germany
has significantly decreased.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.35 / 28 September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
At the same time, France benefits from cheaper electricity which is also
less sensitive to rising hydrocarbon prices because, in 2025, nearly 95%
of electricity was produced through nuclear and renewable power 4.
Furthermore, electricity accounts for 27% of energy consumption in
France, compared to a European average of 24%.
2. FRANCE: A EUROPEAN LEADER IN SERVICES, INCLUDING TECH, AND IN

UPWARDLY REVISED GROWTH FIGURES
Services account for nearly 80% of French GDP , which is significantly
higher than in the other major Eurozone countries. France is leading
the race within the European Union in several sectors, including finan-
cial services, logistics and tech. This is resulting in a services trade
surplus of nearly 1.7% of GDP , which is offsetting an equivalent external
deficit in goods. This is also behind the investment in intellectual pro-

perty products of around 5.5% of GDP in 2025, the highest rate among
the major European countries.
Furthermore, nearly one in two French people now uses AI, compared
to less than one-third in the United States, according to a study by the
Microsoft AI Economy Institute. France ranks 4 th in the world in this
field. In Europe, it is surpassed only by Ireland (where many compa -
nies in the sector are concentrated). France's combined advantages
(energy and digital culture) place it at the forefront of destinations

for artificial-intelligence investment in Europe, particularly for data
centres (announcements were made during the most recent Choose
France summit).
Additionally, France is the country that revises its growth figures
upwards most frequently. In a recent study, we showed that ex-post re-
visions (as national accounts are only finalised 30 months after the end
of a calendar year) were significant there. This phenomenon is likely
linked to the weighting of tech in the investment of French companies,

with the impact on activity measured more easily along time.
3. THE INDUSTRIAL RECOVERY IS TEMPORARILY SLOWED BY SUPPLY
CONSTRAINTS
The French industrial rebound is real and is supported, in particular,
by strengthening French comparative advantages in aeronautics and
electrical equipment. It relies on rising global demand for these goods
and a European momentum that has been accelerating for several
quarters. As a result, French goods exports increased by 5% in the early

months of 2026 compared to the same period in 2025.
However, the industrial recovery is being hampered by the difficulty
faced by companies in meeting demand. Since the end of the COVID-19
pandemic, industrial production has experienced a bumpy recovery.
The most recent of output surges helped French growth to outperform
between the 2nd and 4th quarters of 2025, when aeronautical production
suddenly increased by nearly 20%. The increased delivery targets of

the major companies in the sector could have driven further growth.
This did not materialise, as manufacturing production decreased by
1.2% over the last three months for which data has been published
(May–July) compared to the previous three months.
Production for companies with the most filled order books (aeronau -
tics, electrical and electronic equipment) has been limited by a sharp
rise in supply constraints. The production capacity of industrial com -

panies puts them in a position to meet demand less effectively than
before, due to both shortages of skilled labour and an insufficient nu -
mber of production lines. This situation calls for more investment. The
good news is that, according to the latest quarterly survey from Insee,
investment intentions in the industry have rebounded significantly.
4 With the exception of 2022 (maintenance of nuclear power plants).
4. STRONG BUSINESS CREATIONS SUPPORT EMPLOYMENT

When France undergoes a period of recession or low growth, business
creation also tends to dive. This was the case during the Eurozone
crisis in the first half of the 2010s, but that is not what we are seeing
in 2026. Business creation has even seen its growth accelerate since
the beginning of 2026, standing at +12% y/y. It is increasing more si -
gnificantly in digital-related sectors (+46% y/y), which are benefiting
from the wave of AI-related innovation. However, it is also progressing

elsewhere, such as in trade (+13%) and construction (+9%).
Business creation is even increasing faster than business insolvencies
(+5% y/y in the first half), with the rise in insolvencies largely driven by
stronger business creations.

The increase in business creation is noticeable among companies (+5%)
and even more so among micro-enterprises (+16%). This reflects the
rise in self-employment. While payroll employment has seen a slight
decline over the last two years (80,000 net job losses), part of this
slowdown is linked to the increase in self-employment (204,000 net
creations). Overall, France has continued to create jobs and the em -

ployment rate has reached historic highs.
5. FRANCE REMAINS ATTRACTIVE TO FOREIGN INVESTMENT
French has maintained its attractiveness despite the higher uncer -
tainty. Uncertainty increased significantly following the dissolution
of the National Assembly in June 2024, according to estimates from
the Banque de France and Insee. However, France remains at the top
of the European countries in international attractiveness rankings.
The annual EY barometer notes a decrease in the number of projects in

2025 (-17%), but France has maintained its first-place position for the
7th consecutive year. Furthermore, against a European backdrop where
the number of jobs per project has dropped sharply (-25%), it has re -
mained overall stable in France (-4%). This is a sign that, while there
are slightly fewer investments in number, their footprint on the French
economy remains steady.
France's attractiveness is driven in particular by the development of
services, as well as the quality of its infrastructure and logistical capa-

bilities (including transport hubs, often on a European scale). Moreo -
ver, while France already boasts a developed tech sector, the country
is well-positioned to attract foreign investment in AI (low energy costs
and skilled labour).
********
French growth should return in 2027 to a level more in line with its
strengths (our forecast is 1%). Given the prevailing interest rate condi-
tions, this is a necessary condition5 for the public debt ratio to stabilise,

provided that the government succeeds in bringing the fiscal deficit
down to 3% of GDP .
Stéphane Colliac
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com
5 This is also a sufficient condition, provided that the effective interest rate remains below 3.5% (it is expected to stand at 2.1% in 2026 and 3.2% in 2030, according to
our scenario).

THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q3 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 30.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 26.8%. 
 Of the 13 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q3, 84.6% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.7% and prior four quarter average of 81.5%. 
 26Q3 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 11.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 11.2%. 
 76.9% of companies have reported 26Q3 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 63% and an average over the past four quarters of 76.8%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 40 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 65 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 40 by 65 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q4– 27Q3) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.1. 
 During the week of Oct. 5, three S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $773.2B 
from $760.7B). Seven of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since Aug. 1, the materials (-9.1%) and health care (-2.2%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases 
in earnings, while the energy (16.3%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in earnings. Overall, share-
weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 1.6% since the start of the quarter.

Since Aug. 1, the health care (-1.7B) and materials (-1.5B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the information technology (8.3B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 12.5B since the start of the quarter.

13 companies in the S&P 500 Index have reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these companies, 84.6% reported 
earnings above analyst expectations and 7.7% reported earnings below analyst expectations. In a typical quarter 
(since 1994), 67% of companies beat estimates and 20% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 82% of 
companies beat the estimates and 15% missed estimates. 
In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 5.3% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
1994) average surprise factor of 4.4% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 7.7%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 30.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 26.8%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $773.2B in 26Q3, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $591.9B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q3. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q3. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Consumer Staples 
sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (114.7%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $55.1B in 26Q3, 
compared to earnings of $25.7B in 25Q3. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (445.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (116.1%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 25.1%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (66.5%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $256.1B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $153.8B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (135.6%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.9%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 25.3%. 
The Consumer Staples sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $33.7B 
in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $32.6B in 25Q3. 3 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Packaged Foods & Meats (-6.5%) and Household Products (-4.2%) sub-industries have 
the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 6.4%.

weighted earnings of $625.7B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q4. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q4. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Real Estate sector 
has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q4. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (102.8%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $49.2B in 26Q4, 
compared to earnings of $24.3B in 25Q4. 3 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (230.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (129.3%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 39.4%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (54.9%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $289.1B in 26Q4, compared to earnings of $186.6B in 25Q4. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (117%) and Semiconductor Materials &

the growth rate declines to 18.4%. 
The Real Estate sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (4.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $15.2B in 
26Q4, compared to earnings of $14.6B in 25Q4. 2 of the 13 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Telecom Tower Reits (-13.3%) and Multi-Family Residential Reits (-0.1%) sub-industries 
have the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 7.1%.

improves to 5.1%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted

average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate),
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time.
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.

StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.
Over the next two weeks, 31 S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, three positive

surprises and five negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

## Document 310: Morning Report (2026-10-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Morning Report (2026-10-02).pdf

Date: 2026-10-02

Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Markets spent the session 
navigating a familiar mix 
of shifting geopolitical 
headlines, evolving central 
bank expectations and 
lingering uncertainty ahead 
of tonight’s US nonfarm 
payrolls report. 
Fed Vice Chair Jefferson 
added to a growing chorus 
of Fed officials tempering 
expectations of a back-
to-back FOMC rate hike, 
sparking a rally in Treasuries 
and seeing markets slash 
the probability of an October 
move from around 70% at the 
start of the week to just 30%. 
Risk sentiment was mixed 
across equity markets, with 
US shares recovering into 
the close and Asian bourses 
posting solid gains, though 
European markets and 
the ASX 200 came under 
renewed selling pressure. 
Commodity markets remain 
sensitive to developments in 
the Middle East, as reports of 
oil flows returning towards 
pre-war levels briefly weighed 
on prices before renewed 
conflict concerns lifted Brent 
above US$100/bbl.

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
TODAY’S INSIGHTS
Financial Markets
•  Share markets were mixed globally. In the US, risk sentiment
started to recover in late NY trading, seeing the S&P 500 rise
0.2% while other major indices (Dow Jones, NASDAQ) were
broadly steady. Across the pond, however, the Euro Stoxx

50 and FTSE 100 closed 1.5% and 1.7% lower respectively,
capturing the brunt of the weakness in risk sentiment in
trading overnight.
• Asian share markets traded a more constructive tone
yesterday, following earlier momentum from the US. Most
notably, Tokyo extended its rebound with another large
gain owing to chipmakers (+3.3%) and Seoul bounced
higher (+2.0%). Chinese markets were closed. The ASX 200
disappointed with a 2.0% decline, with all sectors in the red.

•  Treasuries rallied following comments from the Fed Vice
Chair Williams which downplayed expectations of a back-to-
back rate hike from the FOMC. The resulting bull steepening,
with 2-year yields down 10bps and 10-year yields down
4bps, continues to see the 2-10 spread widen sharply.
Markets are now pricing in just a 30% chance of an October
rate hike, compared to 70% at the start of the week.
• Yields also fell across Europe and the UK, with the 10Y Bund

and Gilt down 8bps and 3bps respectively. ACGBs meanwhile
sold off during local trading, though the move looks to have
largely unwound in futures trading overnight. Swaps markets
continue to see at least one more rate hike from the RBA,
though it is fully priced in for early 2027.
•  The USD continued rally overnight, the DXY index finishing
0.6% higher and grabbing a foothold around the 102 level.
A sharp pull-back in the Euro was particularly eye-catching,

strong selling pressure driving it down 0.8% to the mid
USD1.12 range, the weakest since May 2025. The Sterling
and Yen also both weakened against the greenback, down
0.5% and 0.4% respectively. The Aussie dollar was a bit more
contained overnight given the extended pull-back so far this
week, edging another 0.2% lower to USD0.6930.
•  Crude oil markets were much more lively yesterday. Prices
dropped sharply during Asian trading, on reports that flows

of crude oil (including estimates so-called ‘dark’ transit
through the Strait) are very close to pre-war levels. However,
that optimism quickly faded, with concerns about active
conflict being revived following reports that the US is sending
a third aircraft carrier strike group to the region. Brent rose
4.9% to $102.8/bbl.
• Base metals retreated against the backdrop of higher oil
prices and a firmer USD, with LME copper down 1.2%
to around US$14,244/mt, the lowest level since mid-

September. Gold prices meanwhile rose 0.5% to around
US$4,180/oz, buoyed by a lift in real yields.
International Data
In the US, the ISM Manufacturing PMI  was consistent with
ongoing expansion in the sector, the index broadly stable
at 54.5 in September. The detail suggests demand remains
robust, with new orders and order backlogs increasing in
the month. However, the gauge tracking input prices rose to
its highest since May all while supplier delivery times have

continued to increase, suggesting supply chain stresses remain
a key concern. Manufacturers are also growing headcount.
This is consistent with an ongoing low level of initial jobless
claims which held at 197k last week.
Overnight, the FOMC’s Jefferson  played down the risk of a
back-to-back rate hike, stating “any future adjustments in
policy should be determined by carefully examining trends
in the data, the evolving outlook, and balance of risks” and

that he and his colleagues “will need to come to our own
judgement, which may take more time”. Similarly, the FOMC’s
Bowman stated that she does not currently see “an urgent
need for further action”.
In Europe, the unemployment rate  held steady at 6.4% in
August, only slightly above its historic low of 6.2% in 2024,
indicating a solid labour market.
Local Data
In Australia, the goods trade surplus narrowed from $1.4bn
to $0.5bn in August, driven by another surge in imports for

data centre-related equipment, amid ongoing volatility in
gold trade (see here). Meanwhile, Cotality dwelling prices
fell 1.1% nationwide in September, with the detail suggesting
the price correction is both deepening and broadening across
markets (see here).

## Document 342: Thanks For The Memories

Source PDF: all report pdf/Thanks For The Memories.pdf
Report source: Substack Reports
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Thanks For The Memories
Date:    4 hours ago
Thanks For The Memories

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Thanks For The Memories
By Ed Yardeni & 2 others • 1 Oct 2026
View in browser

I. Micron and the AI Trade
Memory has become one of the biggest beneficiaries of the AI spending
boom. Micron’s stock, for example, is up 273.2% ytd, second only to
SanDisk among the semiconductor companies. We have been bullish on
memory, and Micron’s fiscal Q4-2026 results reinforced our view. Revenue
surged 379% y/y to a record $54.2 billion, above the $50.8 billion
consensus. Adjusted EPS of $33.42 topped management’s guidance
range. Gross margins widened to 87% as DRAM and NAND prices
surged.

### Page 2

The outlook was even stronger. Micron guided fiscal Q1-2027 revenue to
$61.5 billion, well above the roughly $57 billion analysts expected.
Management expects memory supply-demand conditions to tighten further
in 2027 and 2028, with more than 75% of fiscal 2027 output already
committed. The company now has 26 long-term agreements (LTAs), up
from 16 last quarter.
Many investors still view memory as a classic boom-bust commodity.
Before the latest earnings report, Micron’s forward P/E was 6.8. The stock

barely budged today following the company's gangbusters results (chart).
Micron’s results reinforce the case that the AI buildout still has plenty of
runway. Hyperscalers continue to increase infrastructure spending.
Indeed, Goldman Sachs recently raised its 2027 hyperscaler capex
forecast to $1.2 trillion, roughly 50% above $750 billion this year.
II. "Growthflation"
With the AI buildout continuing to act as a powerful private-sector stimulus
program, it is hardly surprising that the latest indicators continue to point

to a strong economy. At the same time, inflation remains stuck around
3.0%. Today, the 10-year Treasury yield briefly hit 5.33% and the 30-year
reached 5.68%, both marking fresh 24-year highs, before easing back a
bit (chart). The message is clear: robust growth and persistent inflation are
putting upward pressure on long-term yields.

### Page 3

We think the bond yield is reflecting the strength of nominal GDP, which
rose 6.3% y/y during Q2 (chart). We don't expect the bond yield to
depress the economy unless it surges above the growth rate of nominal
GDP, which we don't expect to happen. In fact, Dr Ed visited our
institutional accounts in Toronto over the past two days and found that all
of them expressed interest in buying bonds at these attractive yields.
Now, consider the latest evidence of the "growthflation" driving nominal

GDP:
(1) Labor market. Initial jobless claims fell to 197,000 during the week of
September 25, the lowest level since July 18 (chart). They have been
below 200,000 for seven weeks this year, the most in any year since

### Page 4

1969. Additionally, the latest reading is near a 57-year low. Continuing
claims dropped to 1,701,000, the lowest reading since March 2023.
Challenger job-cut announcements fell 18% m/m and 20% y/y to 43,281 in
September, the lowest total for the month since 2022 (chart). Layoff
activity remains subdued across the US economy.
Private payroll growth also appears to be firming. ADP employment rose
by 90,000 in September, while Revelio Labs estimates that private

payrolls increased by 56,900 last month (chart). We expect tomorrow’s
BLS report to show roughly 100,000 nonfarm payroll gains in September,
more than enough to keep the unemployment rate near its current low of
4.1%.

### Page 5

(2) Construction spending. Construction spending jumped 0.9% m/m in
August to a $2.20 trillion annual rate (chart).
Private nonresidential construction rose 1.0% to $773 billion, while
residential construction increased 1.1% to $882 billion (chart). Office
construction, which includes data centers, surged 4.6% m/m, while power
construction rose 0.9%. Both categories continue to benefit from the
massive AI infrastructure buildout.

### Page 6

(3) Manufacturing. The ISM M-PMI remained firmly in expansion territory
at 54.5 in September, marking the ninth consecutive month above 50.0
and the longest expansion streak since 2022. Major components,
including new orders, production, and employment, also remained in
expansion territory. The upswing is being fueled by the AI capex boom,
reshoring, and stronger incentives for domestic investment, including
immediate expensing under the OBBBA.
Meanwhile, input cost pressures remained elevated in September. The

ISM prices-paid index rose to 77.9, near its highest level since 2022, while
regional Fed price surveys also remained high (chart).
Respondent comments in the ISM survey noted that demand remains
strong in semiconductors, electronics, machinery, and AI/data-center-

### Page 7

clause.
